## 2023-04-04

> Instead of treating AGI as a binary threshold, I prefer to treat it as a continuous spectrum defined by comparison to time-limited humans.
> 
> I call a system a t-AGI if, on most cognitive tasks, it beats most human experts who are given time t to perform the task.
> 
> More details:

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1643310525697105935 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230408201242/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1643310525697105935?s=20)

## 2023-04-02 · reply to @adamdangelo

> @adamdangelo @ESYudkowsky @moskov @ylecun @robbensinger Based on my debate with Eliezer I'd strongly recommend that in this type of communication he only tries to defend (say) a 30% or 50% probability of catastrophe. Defending higher numbers sharply increases the inferential gap without changing the research/policy implications much.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1642638985557155842 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230403004644/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1642638985557155842)

## 2023-04-02 · reply to @ylecun

> @ylecun @anthrupad Most of these people have existential risk estimates for the next couple of decades higher than Stuart's iirc. I certainly have beliefs that would (and do) make many others terrified, I'm just temperamentally a fairly relaxed person. Seems better to focus on beliefs not emotions.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1642413807032061954 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20240223101626/https://mobile.twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1642413807032061954)

## 2023-03-30 · reply to @jachiam0

> @jachiam0 EVERY person who wants the police to arrest people who break the law is in favor of violence. But advocating for laws is not advocating for violence. The same is true about advocating for international treaties.
> 
> You're creating the meme we're both trying to prevent. Please stop.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1641279362682228737 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20240223093814/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1641279362682228737)

## 2023-03-29 · reply to @hendrycks

> @hendrycks For the record I disagree with this argument. Evolutionary forces are very slow compared to deliberate design or gradient descent. Plus governments, norms, culture, + other power structures all push against "survival of the fittest". So this frame obscures more than it clarifies.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1641215017776467970 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20240613020402/https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1641215017776467970)

## 2023-03-28 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> The best humans will still be better (tho much slower) at:
> - writing novels
> - robustly pursuing a plan over multiple days
> - generating scientific breakthroughs, including novel theorems (tho NNs will have proved at least 1)
> - typical manual labor tasks (vs NNs controlling robots)

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1640568778814816258 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20250105223732/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1640568778814816258?s=20)

## 2023-03-28 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> - autonomously design, code and distribute whole apps (but not the most complex ones)
> - beat any human on any computer task a typical white-collar worker can do in 10 minutes
> - write award-winning short stories and publishable 50k-word books
> - generate coherent 20-min films

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1640568776495353860 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230330073417/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1640568776495353860?s=20)

## 2023-03-20 · reply to @togelius

> @togelius Where are the critics? I've been keeping an eye out for a long time, and just can't find much. There's this piece by LeCun, and the Chollet piece. In both cases they make some interesting points and also miss even the most basic arguments that alignment researchers have given.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1637929456231915520 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230321010536/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1637929456231915520)

## 2023-03-20

> The way that the most prominent critics of AI risk totally fail to engage with even the most basic arguments made by people in the field suggests that they don't have any good counterarguments. That's very concerning!

Quoting https://x.com/hardmaru/status/1637728577331544065:
> “Don’t Fear the Terminator”
> 
> Artificial intelligence never needed to evolve, so it didn’t develop the survival instinct that leads to the impulse to dominate others.
> 
> Article by @TonyZador @YLeCun
> https://t.co/mOecitESkG

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1637737722541658117 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230321010502/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1637737722541658117?s=20)

## 2023-02-16

> It's pretty crazy to have followed the fast takeoff vs slow takeoff debate in the abstract for a decade, and then... a slow takeoff... just starts happening...

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1626328799318245376 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230420010112/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1626328799318245376)

## 2023-01-28 · reply to @geoffanders

> @geoffanders Objective moral truths: my best operationalisation is something like "preferences all reasonable minds would converge to", which psychology addresses.
> 
> Mind: I expect that if you understand how consciousness is *implemented* well enough, then the "hard problem" dissolves.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1619127258811363330 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230323111456/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1619127258811363330)

## 2023-01-27 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Confusions in philosophy of mind could be solved by deeply understanding how consciousness is implemented.
> 
> Including the "hard problem", because better theories don't just answer questions, they also dissolve them in ways that we can't imagine before the theory exists.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1619030208413331457 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230129154136/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1619030208413331457)

## 2023-01-18 · reply to @togelius

> @togelius @ozziegooen @mitpress @ESYudkowsky A framing that is accessible to non-technical readers and has also resonated with many ML researchers I've talked to is the idea of emergent risks at scale: see https://t.co/cwJXlutpHI and https://t.co/L4TJ3sjb2K.
> 
> I use that framing in my paper: https://t.co/YTxGgRrJKB

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1615797730055716864 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230126234647/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1615797730055716864)

## 2023-01-07

> This is a good thread, but we shouldn't be giving oxygen to transparently false claims that large language models lack any understanding of the world.
> 
> What we *should* be trying to figure out is the extent to which they have internally-represented goals or situational awareness.

Quoting https://x.com/labenz/status/1611724185709027328:
> as an AI obsessive and long-time @ezraklein fan, I was excited to see yesterday's podcast with @GaryMarcus.
> 
> Unfortunately, as I listened, my excitement gave way to frustration, and I felt compelled to write my first-ever megathread. 
> 
> Quotes from: https://t.co/ePWGXvaoMc

## 2022-08-31 · reply to @catherineols

> @catherineols @jamespayor @Meaningness I'm confused about the "in the limit" bit, since the whole point of my analogy is that eventually the road curves and you fall off.
> 
> And I do think more alignment produces *some* increase in demand, my point is just that deceptiveness would make this effect pretty small.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564834407319425029 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220831051656/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564834407319425029)

## 2022-08-31 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> @catherineols @jamespayor @Meaningness This is basically just the deceptive alignment argument.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564829854255566848 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220831041848/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564829854255566848)

## 2022-08-31 · reply to @catherineols

> @catherineols @jamespayor @Meaningness I'll try bikes. When you ride a bike with no handlebars slowly, you fall off. As you speed up, you get more stable - but you still can't turn! And once bikes are stable with or without handlebars, the lack of handlebars is unlikely to limit biking much while roads are straight.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564829639914074112 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220831041726/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564829639914074112)

## 2022-08-31 · reply to @catherineols

> @catherineols @jamespayor @Meaningness This argument is reasonable but I have a hard time seeing how it could plausibly outweigh the first-order effect of increasing alignment, since misalignment will become increasingly difficult to detect in increasingly sophisticated systems.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564820810078232577 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220831094040/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564820810078232577)

## 2022-08-30 · reply to @Meaningness

> @Meaningness In this case it'd be ideal to have both careful credences and also strong advocacy - once you're at double digit credence in existential risk from AI, you should be very strongly pushing for alignment progress. But it seems like human brains can mostly only do one or the other.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564682884513800193 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220830183531/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564682884513800193)

## 2022-08-29 · reply to @ShareAnt1

> @ShareAnt1 If you think this is a problem, the government is free to build new housing and allocate it however it likes.
> 
> But the best way to remove the advantages of current landlords is building more housing to compete with them. That's why landlords keep blocking new development.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564314395542360064 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220829195029/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564314395542360064)

## 2022-08-29 · reply to @ShareAnt1

> @ShareAnt1 It sounds like you agree that we need to build new housing, and you just want a bunch of other stuff to happen too. But when governments try to do the other stuff, it ends up with ridiculous consequences like in this thread. So let's just skip that and build new housing directly.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564294535068299264 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220829180211/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1564294535068299264)

## 2022-08-20

> Figuring out the best way to explain fundamental concepts is generally very underrated. I've been doing a lot of thinking of this type recently, especially in designing my alignment curriculum. Will add this as an optional lecture there - thanks Andrej!

Quoting https://x.com/karpathy/status/1559672719414681601:

## 2022-08-17 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> How can we prevent AGI misalignment? I'm most excited about work on scalable oversight (theory: https://t.co/LZqCPtcD0D; practice: https://t.co/i3HreKCpyf) and mechanistic interpretability (https://t.co/Jq68YXXAas). See this curriculum for more: https://t.co/FZklkurzz7

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991268146524160 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220818011434/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991268146524160)

## 2022-08-17 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Fundamentally these are claims about inductive biases, which wouldn't apply to a lookup-table "AGI". But realistic AGIs will do instrumental reasoning, which will bias them towards power-seeking generalization - like preventing us from shutting them down. https://t.co/moFhCP0Rcq

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991263973126144 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220817195232/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991263973126144)

## 2022-08-17 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> We'll try to train AGIs to follow constraints like honesty, but that won't scale as they become superhuman. Analogously, an adult constrained to be honest can still persuade a child to approve of actions which are very harmful, like eating food which happens to be poisonous.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991260118519808 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220818054344/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991260118519808)

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991258252095488 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220817205722/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991258252095488)

## 2022-08-17 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Situationally-aware policies will be able to get higher reward by deliberately exploiting our mistakes - e.g. by lying about their goals, trying to earn our trust, etc. Advanced AIs will be very good at modelling humans (there's a lot of data!) so this will be hard to catch.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991250597449729 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220817195241/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1559991250597449729)

## 2022-08-17 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Advantages of one-off selection:
> - once in, people can feel secure and abundant, which helps them do great things
> - the best people are self-motivated
> 
> Disadvantages:
> - the stronger the selection, the more performance on the proxy diverges from what you care about
> - people change

## 2022-08-17

> A question on my mind: when you're optimizing for a metric using imperfect proxies, when is it better to do one-off strong selection, vs continual weak selection?

> This frame makes slow updating more rational. Coherent decision-making is hard when using a worldview with many exceptions or poor moral foundations. When it's hard to synthesize a new combined worldview you should often still bet on your current one. https://t.co/HVNC3C4VL3

Quoting https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1547415806501392386:
> EAs often debate epistemic modesty: given that there are usually many people better-informed than you, why should you back your own worldview over theirs?
> 
> I argue that this problem dissolves when you combine worldviews' decisions not their beliefs.
> https://t.co/mJwrN38AQ1

## 2022-07-10 · reply to @MikePFrank

> @MikePFrank @davidad @robbensinger @Learner69420 @KerryLVaughan @bayeslord @BasedBeff Do you have the same position when it comes to reducing the probability of nuclear war wiping everyone out?
> 
> (Also, we *could* be in a position to say "oh man, the nukes/AGI weapons have launched, we don't have long now...)

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546150355901227008 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220710151325/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546150355901227008)

## 2022-07-10 · reply to @MikePFrank

> @MikePFrank @robbensinger @davidad @Learner69420 @KerryLVaughan @bayeslord @BasedBeff I think the stability of monocultures is, unfortunately, a contingent fact that depends on technology level. E.g. monocultures become much more stable given surveillance tech, cloning, lie detection, etc, in addition to AGI, which all seem plausible in not too long.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546148626858213381 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220710150604/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546148626858213381)

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546128418948907009 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220710134543/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546128418948907009)

## 2022-07-10 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> @ben_j_todd (also note that adopting the empirical beliefs that many EAs have about AI makes empowering one's altruistic side not too different from empowering one's selfish side, since both would lead to a heavy focus on AGI alignment)

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546113857810300932 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220710124747/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546113857810300932)

## 2022-07-10 · reply to @ben_j_todd

> @ben_j_todd Again, I disagree with this frame. Level of altruism is not an exogenous variable - it's not a coincidence that many people join EA then commit more of their lives to altruism. And if EA does political advocacy it will need to formulate moral demands, like "end factory farming".

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546112364885549056 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220710124241/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546112364885549056)

## 2022-07-10 · reply to @ben_j_todd

> @ben_j_todd Hmm, this frame feels weird. Caring about others and caring about yourself seem less like "options" and more like values you have. Doing both is less like setting a cap and more like negotiating a compromise between multiple parts. More a psychological than philosophical problem.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546110645288017920 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220710123602/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1546110645288017920)

## 2022-07-09 · reply to @jeffrsebo

> @jeffrsebo @tyler_m_john I think "build as good a world as possible" has collaborative connotations which "counterfactual improvement" doesn't have. Evaluating counterfactuals in the context of a group is weird, and I expect you can easily screw it up with a naive account of counterfactual improvement.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545812662738616320 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220709172631/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545812662738616320)

## 2022-07-08 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> @robbensinger @Zergylord @dfrsrchtwts @FelixHill84 @ESYudkowsky @MIRIBerkeley @JacobSteinhardt My read is that Rob is answering #2, as posed in Steven's original tweet. https://t.co/8Nzb24mLU8
> 
> But it sounds like Steven might now be asking: why not set up alignment-specific peer reviewed venues? In which case my main answer is: everyone's been too busy so far!

Quoting https://x.com/Zergylord/status/1544459826494939136:
> Why don't AI alignment/safety/x-risk folks publish in peer-reviewed venues more often?

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545427240644620288 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220708151948/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545427240644620288)

## 2022-07-08 · reply to @Zergylord

> @Zergylord @FelixHill84 @ESYudkowsky @MIRIBerkeley @JacobSteinhardt I agree we can make rigorous claims about *some* nonexistent things, the difficulty is making rigorous claims about specific topics of interest. We have proofs about AIXI, but are they informative about realistic AGI? Apparently not, if wikipedia reports Jan's views accurately: https://t.co/TnuMvt1JLX

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545425056192270336 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220708151128/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545425056192270336)

## 2022-07-07 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> @iamtrask @Zergylord @FelixHill84 @ESYudkowsky @MIRIBerkeley @JacobSteinhardt E.g. Langosco et al on goal misgeneralization (ICML 2022) draws heavily from Risks from Learned Optimization (not peer-reviewed). Alignment-specific peer review likely would've improved RfLO; ICML review would've cut too much. https://t.co/W0RX49OZj3
> https://t.co/0uJJpIZLzi

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545178976372523013 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220707225409/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545178976372523013)

## 2022-07-07 · reply to @iamtrask

> @iamtrask @Zergylord @FelixHill84 @ESYudkowsky @MIRIBerkeley @JacobSteinhardt Oh, if you're talking about peer review in general, I totally agree this would be beneficial. I was only arguing against forms of peer review that would apply the usual ML criteria for and standards of rigor to all alignment work.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545176268336922630 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220707224249/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1545176268336922630)

## 2022-07-07 · reply to @iamtrask

> @iamtrask @Zergylord @FelixHill84 @ESYudkowsky @MIRIBerkeley @JacobSteinhardt I think the main ones are:
> - developing techniques with clear/intuitive arguments for why they plausibly scale up to AGI
> - developing research ideas that are hard to communicate to other people
>

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1526972994400051200 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220518170904/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1526972994400051200)

## 2022-05-18 · reply to @ThinkingBone

> @ThinkingBone This sounds like a very "Age of surveillance capitalism" take; see my critique of this line of thinking here: https://t.co/GsF48z5iTI

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1526736072415227904 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220518012723/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1526736072415227904)

## 2022-05-17 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Moore's law, and the copyability of software, are the two biggest drivers of this difference. But we also shouldn't underestimate the role of ad-based revenue models, which make price discrimination much easier. https://t.co/guwCndGXu8

Quoting https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1516883042920538114:
> Hypothesis: Google doesn't let people pay for ad-free search because some users are worth way more to advertisers than any reasonable subscription price, and those are the ones disproportionately likely to sign up for ad-free search.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1526692173906735104 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220517223241/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1526692173906735104)

## 2022-04-23 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Both nature and nurture affect each example. Why do they update me different ways?
> - Amanda and I are surprisingly similar even in our shared cultural niche
> - childhood is where nurture effects are strongest, and I find reading lots as an adult a weaker predictor of compatibility

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1517893829684342785 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220423155203/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1517893829684342785)

## 2022-04-23

> The most compelling evidence on nature vs nurture from my personal life:
> - pro-nature: @manda_ngo and I keep converging towards the same beliefs, traits, and projects
> - pro-nurture: I have never met a person who also read a ridiculous amount during childhood, who I didn't like

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1517890954816069635 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220423154018/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1517890954816069635)

## 2022-04-23 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Btw, this thread is mostly about EA, but I think generalises pretty well. E.g. academia is so nitpicky and gatekeepy in part as a defense against sliding into bullshit. It worked for a while, but is failing now because so few people are trying to improve the system as a whole.

> Your regular reminder that there are enormous losses which could be prevented by incredibly simple policy changes - most notably on housing, immigration, and energy.
> 
> Once you've internalised that, it's easier to believe that we're totally dropping the ball on emerging tech too.

## 2022-04-22

> Doctor, you say there are no halos
> around the streetlights in Paris
> and what I see is an aberration
> caused by old age, an affliction.
> 
> I tell you I will not return to a universe
> of objects that don’t know each other,
> as if islands were not the lost children
> of one great continent

Quoting https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/1517216002865602560:
> There's a kind of strength I value that isn't about how powerful your defenses are, but allowing yourself to become shattered. I'd rather be in connection and broken than disconnected and whole.

> Good thread, a couple of additions:
> 1. When replicating papers, pick whichever will teach you most. Start very simple, much better if it takes too little time and you need to try another than if you get stuck halfway. Don't start with RL or alignment papers, they're harder.

## 2022-04-12 · reply to @daniellellecco

## 2022-04-11 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> To be clear, the arguments for worrying about AGI don't rely on claims about current models' capabilities (see https://t.co/ugE4imXHPO).
> 
> But it's important to track what you actually expected a few years ago, and what would change your mind now. Otherwise you'll get frog-boiled.

Quoting https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1509416046779723778:
> My first ever podcast episode, with @dfrsrchtwts, is now out! We talk through the alignment problem from the ground up; I think there'll be something interesting in there no matter your current level of familiarity.
> https://t.co/aCS28uRmeF

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1513594235257196547 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220411190647/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1513594235257196547)

## 2022-04-11

> If you were dropped back in time into the body of your childhood self, what aspects of the world would you try hardest to influence, and how?

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1513547555585495042 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220411160315/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1513547555585495042)

## 2022-04-11 · possibly deleted

> This is a good point. But remember that a little bit of prompt engineering goes a long way. When I ask the question in the original form, GPT-3 struggled. When I phrased it as below, it got it very easily. Proving it doesn't know something is hard!
> https://t.co/qzH1pJvsjh

## 2022-04-11 · reply to @imperialauditor

> @imperialauditor Probably mainstream software engineering. Also, with a later intervention, probably I'd have kept doing ML research engineering instead of my current forecasting/governance work.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1513316796694085632 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220411004359/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1513316796694085632)

## 2022-04-11 · reply to @michael_nielsen

> @michael_nielsen @MelMitchell1 Makes sense. I think the part I'm most interested in is the emergence of reasoning skills (and related traits like compositionality). I hadn't expected that evaluating the extent to which models can "actually do X" would be so difficult for so many X.

> 
> Best to start with adults - I'm guessing people in their 20s retain enough neuroplasticity to make interesting things happen.

Quoting https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1293286668817043470:
> You know how the visual cortex of blind infants rewires itself to process sound or touch? What if you hooked up their optic nerves to the internet instead? Imagine reading a page of text in a second, as easily as others recognise objects visually. The first true digital native.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1466480188309524481 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20211202185403/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1466480188309524481)

## 2021-11-25 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> Fitness: is only defined locally, with respect to a population
> 
> GDP: same, GDP comparisons over time are very hacky
> 
> Welfare: you can't untangle selfish vs altruistic preferences
> 
> International law: see IR realism
> 
> The scientific method: *what* scientific method? See Strevens

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1463976718134231051 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20211126151346/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1463976718134231051)

## 2021-11-25

> Ten abstractions that turned out to be much less robust than I'd expected:
> - Evolutionary fitness
> - GDP
> - Economic welfare
> - International law
> - The scientific method
> - Pleasure/suffering
> - Personal identity
> - Most ML theory
> - Planning
> - Rationality
> 
> Brief explanations ⬇️

## 2020-10-21 · reply to @RichardMCNgo

> 4 premises:
> 1. We’ll build AIs which are much more intelligent than humans.
> 2. Those AIs will be autonomous agents which pursue large-scale goals.
> 3. Those goals will be misaligned with ours.
> 4. The development of such AIs would lead to them gaining control of humanity’s future.

https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1319008799248834560 · [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201021201404/https://twitter.com/RichardMCNgo/status/1319008799248834560)
